नई दिल्ली , 8 नवम्बर, 1968 
एस० प्रो० 4120 -यस खाच अपमिश्रण निवारण अधिनियम 1954 ( 1954 का 37 ) की 
धारा 3 की उप-धारा ( 2 ) के खण्ड ( घ ) का पालन करते हुए केन्द्रीय सरकार ने रेल मनालय 
(रेलवे बोर्ड ) , नई दिल्ली में निर्दशक ( स्वास्थ्य ) एस० टी० ओ० बनवारी लाल का खाध मानको की 
केन्द्रीय समिति में एक सदस्य के रूप में पुनः नामित किया है । , 


और यतः इस अधिनियम की धारा 3 की उप-धारा 2 के खण्ड ( क ) को पालन करते 
ईए पश्चिम बंगाल राज्य सरकार ने केन्द्रीय संयुक्त प्रयोगशाला पलवस के लोषविष ( ख द्य 
एवं जल ) श्री एस० सी० पक्रटत., एम० एस० सी० डी० फिल० के खध मारकोक य 
समिति में अपने प्रतिनिधित्व के लिए एक सदस्य के रूप में पुनः नामित किया है ; 

भत जब उपत अधिनियम की धारा के उप-धारा ( 1 ) रप्र यिंपाप्रयंर वरते 
हुए केन्द्रीय सरकार एतदद्वारा निदेश देती है कि रेल मंत्रालय ( रेलवे बोर्ड ) नई दिल में कि 
( स्वास्थ्य ) 10 बनवारी लाल तथा केन्द्रीय संयुक्त प्रयोगशाली लयत में लेप विहिक 
( बाय तथा जल ) डा० एस० सी० चक्रवर्ती, एम० एस० सी०डी० फिल० केन्द्रीय खाध मानक . 
समिति के सदस्य बने रहेंगे ।

9 . 0 . 4145 , - In exercise of the powers conferred by clause (a ) of sub -section (3 ) of 
section 17 of the Employees Provident Funds Act, 1952 ( 19 of 1932 ) , and in supersession 
of the notifcation of the Government of India , in the Ministry of Labour, Employment 
and Rehabilitation (Dopartment of Labour and Employment ) No. S . O . 3368 dated tho 
15th September, 1967, the Central Government hereby directs that every cmployer in 
relation to an establishment exempted under clause ( a ) or clause (b ) of sub -section ( 1 ) 
of section 17 of the said Act or in relation to an employee or a class of employces exemp 
ted under paragraph 27 or as the case may be , paragruph 27 A of the Employees Provident 
Funds Schenie , 1952 shall transfer the monthly provident fund contributions within fifteen 
days of the close of the month to the Board of Trustees , duly constituted in respect of 
that establishment, and that the said Board of Trustees shall invest every month , within a 
period of two weeks from the date of receipt of the said amounts from the employer, the 
provident fund accumulations , that is to say the contributions , inteiest and sundry roceipts 
as reduced by any obligatory outgoings in accordance with the following pattern , 
namely : 
(i) in securities created and issued by State Government and other securities gua 

ranteed by the Central or Stato Government — not exceeding 35 per cent . 
( ii ) The balance in Central Government socurities including the savings or other 

certificates issued by the Central Government. 
The above pattern will be in force for the period on and from the 1st September , 1968 , 
to the 31st March , 1969 , 

All re - investment of provident fund Accumulations (whether invested in securities creat 
ed and issued by the Central Government or in savings cortificates issued by the Central 
Government or in sccurities created and issued by a State Government) shall also be 
made according to the pattern mentioned in para 1 alyove . 

3 . The Board of Trustees shall formulate propor proceduro for prompt investment 
reinvestment of accumulations in accordance with the aforesaid directions and shall have 
it approved by the Regional Provident Fund Commissioner concerned .

Dhanbud , dated the 24th October, 1968 

AWARD 
Tlio Central Government, in the Ministry of Labour, Employment and Rehabilitation , 
has , under its order No. 20 / 4 /68-LRI dated the 22nd June, 1968, made this reference to 
this Tribunal for adjudication of an industrial dispute described in the schedule as 
follows: 


SCHEDULE 


" Whether the management of Messrs A . K . Guplu s Mica Concern, Owner of Sqr. 

No. 35C Mica Mine, was justified in terminating the services of Shri Jugal 
Kishore Prasad , Manager with effect from the 7th February , 1968 ? If not, 
to what relief is he cntitled ?” 


2 . I fixed this case for hearing at Koderma on the 27th September, 1968. On the 26th 
September , 1968, however , I received a petition from the Union , praying for adjournment 
of ite case. I ordered the petition to be put up on the date fixed. When I arrived at 
Koderma Forest Rest House , the venue of the hearing of the case , I found Shri Jagdish 
Prasad , Advocate and Sri Şubrata Kumar Gupta on behalf of the company and Shri Jugal 
Kishore Prasad himself on his own behalf. The union was neither present por represented, 
Shri Jugal Kishore Prasad filed a petition before me personally , praying that the reference 
might bo discharged because he had already received all his dues and he did not wish to 
fight the case with thc company or, in other words , he did not press the casc . I decided 
to fix another date for the hearing of the case so that the union might address me and 
tell me what its attitude in the matter was. So I fixed the 10th October, 1968 for hearing 
of the case at the Forest Rest House , Koderma. I could not get there in time on that 
date due to break - down of my car. Hence I fixed today for further hearing at Dhanbad . 
in the notice which was issued to the union , I mçntioned that, if the union was absent on 
the date fixed , the workmun s application would be considered on merits without hearing 
the union . This notico appears to have been served upon tho union on the 16th October, 
1968. 


3 . Shri Prayag Narain Lal has appeared today on behalf of the employers, The work 
man has not appeared . The union has also not appeared but sent a letter stating that sinco 
the workman has received full satisfaction , it has no objection of his application being 
accepted . In view of the workman s application and in view of the notice given by me, 
therefore , I have to dispose of the case on the basis of the work man s application . Since 
the workman has prayed that the reference may be discharged because he has got full 
satisfaction , I have no alternative but to do so . I accordingly discharge the reference. 
4 . This may be submitted to the Central Government under section 15 of the Act. 

(Sd .) KAMLA SAITAI, 

Presiding Officer 
[No . 20 /4 /69-LRI.)

1947 ) the Central Government hereby publishes the following award of the Central Gov 
ornment Industrial Tribunal, Calcutta in the industrial dispute between the employeis in

was received by the Central Government on the 30th October , 1968. 
CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL , CALCUISA 

REFERENCE No. 60 OF 1967 
PARTIES :

AND 
Thçic workmen .

On bchalf of Workmen .. -Shri Romen Bancrjce , Advocate . 
STATE : West Bengal 

INDUSTRY ; Insurance 

AWARD 
By Order No. 70 ( 9 ) /67- LRIII , dated 26th June 1967, the Government of India , in 
the Ministry of Labour, Employment and Rehabilitation ( Department of Labour and 
Employment ), rcferred the following dispute between the employers in relation to tho 
National Insurance Co. I tu ., Calcutta and their workmen , to this tribunal, for adjudica 
tion , namely : 
" Whether the demands of the workmen of the National Insurance Company 

Limited, Calcutta for payment of House Rent Allowance and for improving 
the cxisting service conditions relating to gratuity , age of retirement and 

sick leave are justified ? If so , to what relief arc they entitled ?" 
2 . The cause of the workinen was espoused by the General Insurance Employees 
Association (hereinafter called the Union ) , which filed a written statement. The 
employer Insurance Company also filed a written statement. 

3 . Tho case tos gratuity as made on behalf of the workmen , in the written statement, 
way that the scheni us prevailing in the company , was unjust, because it entitled an 
employee to gratuity only after 5 years of service , cven in the case of death and per 
mancnt lisability . Further, the benefit was available to il retiring employec only if he 
bad completed 15 years service . Also , on termination of service , on grounds otinis 
conduct, etc ., the employee was deprived of the benefit of gratuity irrespective of the 
gravity of the alleged misconduct and the quantum of loss suffered by the employer cum 
puny as a result thercof. Finally , the maximum limit of 15 months salary , as gratuity , 
wis characterised as unjust, unfair and inadequatc As a provision for old age. 

4 . So far as retireinent age was concerned , the case made on behalf of the work 
men , in the written statement, was that the retircment age at 58 years , as prevailing in 
the employer company, was unjust " in view of the growing life expectancy and the 
trend of current industriul law and practice " . 

5 . So far as sick leave was concerned , the case made on behalf of the workmei, in 
the writica statement, was that 5 days sick /mcdical leave , granted by the employer com 
pacy , was " on the face of it absurd " . 

6 . In respect of house rent allowance, the case made on behalf of the workmen , in 
the writt - 7 tatement, was ihat with admittedly soaring house rents in all suburban 
centres and in the context of absencc of iny housing schemc for the employtes , the 
latter s rral studies are hcing subjected to reduction , thus further lowering down their 
standards. It was further contended that the principle of allowing- house rent to 
(nployees was being growingly recognised by all progressive employers and there was 
no cason why the employer comp. 111 the instant case should not allow some house 
rent allowance . 

7 In rar graphs 1 vnd 4 of the sitten statement, filed on behalf of the workmco , 
it was alleged that the company was in a sounal financial position, was growing in pros 
perit and was in a position to hear the additional burden which may befall by acced . 
ing to the demands of the workmen 


Sec . 3 ( 1 ) ] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : NOV , 23, 1968 / AGRAHAYANA 2 , 1890 5357 


8 . The employer company filed a written statement. So far as the claim for gratuity 
was concerned , it was slated , in paragraph 4 of the written statement, that the gratuity 
scheme in force in the company was quite fair and liberal to the workmen and did not 
warrant any change. It was further stated that the issue should be looked only from 
# " long term view " and should not be interſered with quickly . 


9 . With regard to the claim for increase in the age of retirement, it was stated , in 
paragraph 5 of the written statement , that the existing provision of agc of retiremont 
at 58 years was quite fair to the workmen and in as much as this age of retirement was 
prevalent in large number of concerns and in government establishments , there was 
10 caso for increasing the age of retirement. 

10 . With refcrence to the claim for sick leuve, it was stated , in paragraph 6 of the 
written statement, that the provision lor sick leave should not be considered in isolation . 
It was further stated : 

. the Company allows one month s privilego leave on full pay, 15 days casual 

leave on full pay iind 5 days sick leavo on full pay. Thus the workmen 
are allowed 50 days leave on full pay per year . Further the company is 
also allowing in case of a workman with servico of 10 years or 1101c , in 
the event of serious and prolonged illness when the period is not covci od 
by the sick leave due , it further leave for half the number of days of pri 
vilege Icave which may not have been availed by him during the period 
of service . In addition such 3 workman is also granted further leave at 
half pay upto 1 months and thereafter leave without pay for a further 
period of 9 months . The Company submits that the existing provisions 

of sick leave are quite suficient and liberal, * * * " 
11. With regard to the claim for house rent, it was stated , iti paragraph ; of the 
written statement, that expenses on house rent were included in the emoluments paid 
to the workmen . It was further stated that the issue of pay scales and dearness allow 
Arce was pending in appeal before the Supreme Court and as such the payment of house rent 
upon the claims of the workmen . 


12 . It is in the background of the above stated pleadings that I have to adjudicate 
spon the claims of the workmen . 


13 , On bchalf of the workmen , four witnesses were examined , namely , ( 1) K . S . B . 
Pillai, an employce in General Assurance Society 1.td . Bombay, who is also the Joint 
Secretary of All Indian Insurance Employecs Association , ( ii ) Bhupendra Chandra Dus, 
who is in onowyce rinder Hercules Insurance Company Ltd ., ( ) . P . Ravindiarathan , 
who is i11 enployce under the employer company, the National losirce Company 
Limited and ( iv ) Akrur M . Kundu, who is employee under the British lui General 
Insurance Company Limited . K . S . B , Pillai claims lu have regoliated with S . veral 
insuranCC compuies , in his capacity ils Joint Secretary of All India Insurance Employees " 
Association and also claims to have succeeded in arriving at several dipulit ulic 
tients. The settlements , which he succeeded in bringing about, were :

19 . I need also clear the ground or a doubt expressed by Mr. A . K Dull, Icained 
Advocate for the cmployer company, as tu the extent and scope of the relevacc belore

of the employer company or at the Calcutta branch office or at both the places, I shall,

contention was the language used in the order of reference , namely " Demand of the work

il the workmen of the National insurance Company Limited , both at Calcutta and out

as also at places outyde India . The addition of the word Calvutlu alter the words

Calcutta workmen of the National Inşuiance Company Ltd . If ouly the dispule of the 
Calcutta workinen of the National Insurance Company Limited was intended to be re 
ferred to this tribunal, thien the proper language should have been , " Whether the des 
mund of the workmen at the registered office and branch office of the National Insurance 
Company Limited at Calcutta for payment of house rent allowance and for inproving 
the existing service condition relating to gratuity , age of retirement and sick leave arc 
justificd ?" There are other reasons also which induce me to reject the argument of Mr. 
Duit. It appears from Ext. I that the dispute was raised not by the smaller trade union 
of the Calcuttu employces but by the bigger union of the employees known as the General 
Insurance Employees Association . It also appeurs from Exts. M to that tho 
employees at Madras , Gujarat and Nagpur offices of the employer company adopted the

ulgued that the charter of demand had been submitted as far back as June 1, 1965 and

taken by the employees at Madias, Gujarat and Nagpur omices of the employer company 
were all taken retwcen November , 1965 and July , 1966 , that is to say long after ine re

argue was that tlie cmployees outside Calcutta were never [actually parties to the charler" 
of demand when made but sought to become parties thereto after the same had been 
rejected . According to him , this sort of attempted participation in rejected charter 
would not make the non -Calcutta employecs parties to the dispute. In my opinion , the 
latcı attempts to participate may have been over doing but the original charter of demand 
submitted by the General Insurance Employees Associatior does not appear 10 be limitted 
to the Calcutta employees only . Nor does the language ut Lluc reference , in niy read

trial dispute , in tha instant case , concerns the employees of the National Insurance Com . 
pany Limited as a whole wherever tliey might be serving and did not concern the 
Calculta cmployces only .

5360 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : NOV . 23 , 1968 / AGRAHAYANA 2 , 1890 [PART II — 

- - = = 

= 
The criticism levelled against the existing scheme is four- fold : 
( a ) Injustice behind ineligibility to gratuity before completion of 5 years 

service , even in the case of death or permanent disability of an 

employec. 
(b ) Injustice behind ineligibility of a retiring employce to gratulty hotore 

completion of 15 years of service , 
( c ) Injustice behind ineligibility to gratuity in case of termination of service 

of an cmployce on grounds of misconduct, irrespective of the gravity 

thereof. 

( d ) Unfairness in fixing the maximum limit of gratuity at 15 months salary . 
21. Now , gratuity ordinarily importy the idea of gift or present in return of faithful 
service . Originally , gratuity used to be treated as payment gratutiously made by the 
employer to the employee , at its pleasure . At present, however , gratuity is regarded as 
a legitimate benefit which workmen may claim and which , in a proper case , may give 
rise to an industrial dispute . In the case of Indian Orygen and Acetylene Company 
Limited Employees Union vs Indian O -rygen and Acetylene Company Limited , ( 1956 ) I 
LL ), 435 at page 436 , it was observed : 

. . . . Where an employer company has the financial capacity , the workmen would be 

cntitled to the benefit of gratuity in addition to the benefits of thc Provi 
dent Fund . . . . . . In considering the financial capacity of the concern 
what is to be seen is the general financial stability of the concern . . . . Tho 
factors to be considered before granting a scheme of gratuity are the broad 
aspects of the financial condition of the concern, its profit earning capacity , 
the profit earned in the past, its reserve and the possibility of replenishing 
the reserves , the claim of capital put, having regard to the risk involved , 

in short, the financial stability of the concein ." 
The Supreme Court made the same observations in Express Newspaper ( Private ) 
Limited vs Union of India , ( 1961 ) I LLJ 339 . In a controversy as lo whether tho 
employer has the requisite financial capacity to bear, the burdçn of gratuity , the onus is 
upon the employer to prove the negative, if he wants to escape the burden. This is 
not disputed , 

22 . Up to this stage , 15 years of continuous service was treated as the ininimum 
requisite for eligibility to gratuity on retirement. Later on , however, the Supreme Court 
pointed out that 15 years of continuous service was not a pile of Universal application 
(vide Calcutta Insurance Co . Ltd . vs . their workmen , ( 1967 ) [ LLJ at page 7. Then 
again in the Gurment Cleaning Works ve its workmen , ( 1961) I LII 513 , the question 
which camo up for consideration before the Supreme Court was, whether on award provid 
ing for gratuity on retirement or resignation of a workman after 10 years service at 10 
days consolidated wages for cach year s of service should be upheld . The contention 
put forward on behalf of the cmployer was that the minimum period of service cntitling 
a workman to gratuity should be fixed at 15 years . This contention was icpclled with 
. the following observation ; 
" Gratuity is not paid to the employce gratuitously or merely as a matter of booni. 

It is paid to him for the service rendered by him to tho employer , and 
when it oncc ecined , it is difficult to understand why it should necessarily 
he denied to him whatever may be the nature of misconduct for his dis 
tussal . . . . . . If the misconduct for which the service of an employco is ter 
minated has caused linancial loss to the works, then before gratuity could 
be paid to the employec he is called upon to compensate the employer for 
the whole of the financial loss caused by his misconduct; after this com 
pensation is paid to the employer if any halance from gratuity claimable 
by the cmployec iemains, that is paid to him ." 


The opinion expressed in that case was that gratuity was earned by the enployce for 
long and meritorious service and consequently it should be available to him even though 
at the end of such service he may be found guilty of inisconduct entailing his dismissal. 


23 . On the financial aspect of gratuity scheme, the following observations were mode 
by G . K . Mitter . J, in Calufta Insurance v . Their workmen ( 1967) J LLJ I 
" On the financial aspect of a gratuity scheme, we were referred to the case of 

Wenyer and Co . und rothers y , their workmen ( 1963 ) II LLJ 403. Tliere it 
was observed by this Couit that the problem of the burden imposed by the 
gratuity scheme could be looked at in two ways . One was to capitalize the 
burden on actual basin which would show theoretically that thc burden 
would be very heavy , and the other was to look at the scheme in its practi 
cal aspect and find out how many employees retire every year on the 


SŁc . 3(ii)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : NOV, 23, 1968/ AGRAHAYANA 2, 1890 5361 


average. According to this Court , it was this practical approach which 

ought to be taken into account." 
Further , commenting on Wenger and Co . s case (Supra ) his Lordship said : 
" Further, it was held that the award providing for payment of gratuity for a con 

tinuous service of two years and more , termination of service for whatever 
reason cxcept hy way of dismissal for misconduct involving moral turpitude 
was unduly liberal. This Court ordered deletion of the words involving 
moral turpitude from the provision of gratuity and directed that for ter 
mination of service caused by the employer the minimum period of servico 
for payment of gratuity should be five years and in regard to resignation , 
the employee should be entitled to get gratuity only if he had ten years 

completed service to his crcdit." 
In Calcutta Insurance Co . s case (Supra ) his Lordship further laid down the following 
other principle : 
" In British Pants ( India ) , Ltd . v . its workmen ( 1966 I LLJ 407 ) the tribunal had 

fixed five years minimum service as the qualifying period to enable a work 
man to care gratuity which was payable in case of death or dischar e or 
Yoluntary retirement on rounds of medical unfitncgg or resignation beforo 
reaching the age of superannuation , retirement on reaching the age of 
superannuation or termination of service by the company for reasons Other 
than misconduct resulting in loss to the company in moncy and property . 
In that case the Court obscrved that the reason for providing for a longor 
minimum period for earning gratuity in the case of voluntary retirement or 
resignation was to see that workmen do not leave ono 
concern after another after putting the short minimum service qualify 
ing for gratuity . It was said that a longer minimum in the case of valun 
tary retirenient or resignation makes it more probable that the workmen 
would stick to the company where they were working . Ultimately , this 
Court modified the gratuity scheme and ordered that in the case of 
voluntary retirement or resignation by the employee before reaching the ago 
of superannuation , the minimum period of qualifying service for gratuity 
should be ten years and not five years . 


of 


. 


We do however feel that a workman should not be entitled to any gratuity on 

resignation only after five years of completed and confirmed service and 
that in case of resignation this period should be raised to ten years. We 
also hold , following the principles laid down in the former decisions of this 
Court, that a workman , who is dismissed for misconduct, should be entitled 
to receive gratuity only after completion of fifteen years of service on 
the ground that gratuity is a reward for long and meritorious service , and 
further that, in cases where the misconduct for which the workman is dis 
missed cntailed financial loss to the company , the company would be entitled 

to set off, the loss from the amount of gratuity payable ." 
24 . To gummarise what has been stated in the different Supreme Court judgments , 
referred to above : 

(a ) The benefit of gratuity is available in addition to benefits of Provident Fund. 
( b ) Fifteen years of continuous and meritorious service is too long a period and 

should be reduced to five years in cases of termination of service by the 
employer for reasons other than misconduct causing financial loss to the 
employer and ten years in cases of voluntary retirement before reaching the 

age of superannuation and of resignation , 
( c ) In case of disinissal for misconduct, the workmen should be entitled to receive 

gratuity only on completion of fiftcco years of service and further where 
the misconduct cntails financial loss to the company, the company would be 

entitled to set off the loss against the amount of gratuity payable . 
( 1) The financial capacity of the employer to bear the burden of gratuity should 

he determined by a practical approach to the question , namely lwy deter 

mining how much would become annually payable by way of gratuity. 
To the instant case , there is little evidence to show that the financial burden to be 
thrown on the employer company, by modernising the gratuity scheme will be too heavy . 
It appears from the Director s Report to Sharcholders for the years ending December 
31, 1963, December 31, 1964 , December 31 , 1965, December 31, 1966 and December 
31 , 1967 ( Exts , R . and Exts. 2 to 4 ) , that:

25 . The question that remains for my consideration is whether 15 months basic salary , 
as the maximum gratuity , is much too niygaidly . On this point, the following observa 
tion by the Supreme ( ourt in Hindustan Antibiotics, Ltd . v. Their workmen ( 1967 ) I LLJ 
114 at p . 129 ( per Subba Rao , C . J .) is worthy of consideration : 
" Nor can we agree with the argument of Sri Setalvad that the tribunul should 

have given twenty months basic salary as gratuity . In the nature of 
things , a particular ceiling for gratuity cannot be fixed . It depends upon 
the facts of each case . The tibunal has taken all the relevant circums. 
tances including the fact that the company was contributing to llic 
employces provident ( und at the rate of 8 - 1 / 3 per cent instead of 6 - 1 / 4 per 
cent and also the fuct that pait of the dearness allowance was included in 
the basic wage . We do not see any error of law or anything contrary to 
the practice obtaining in industrial adjudication to compel us to interCerc 
with the details of the said scheme. They are quite fair and cquitable ** * ** 


In this case also , it appears that the employer company contributes 8 - 1 / 3 per cent of the 
basic salary to the Provident Fund ( vide evidence of R . N . Gupta examined by the en 
ployer company ) . It further appears from the recent judgınent of the Supreme Court

workmen ) thut the employer company has to pay dearness allowance at the following 
ratc : 
" Dearness allowance payable to employees in Grade D and E shall be 160 per cent 

of the basic pay as fixed by the Tribuna ). So far as Grade A , B and Care 
concerned the dearness allowance payable to employees in these findes shall 
be 100 per cent on the first one hundred of their basic ray , 80 per cent on 

2nd hundred and 40 per cent on the balance ." 
In most of the memoranda of settlements produced on behali of the union of wothman , 
to which reference has alreacly been made, 15 months basic salary appears to be the 
accepted figure of gratuity for the aloresaid reasons. I do not think that any ground 
for interference with the ceiling fixel for gratuity has been made out. In the view that 
I take, I modify the provision for gratuity , that now exists in the service rules ol the

( ii ) Age of Retirement : 

26 . The age of retirement, under the service rules is 58 years , with libcrty to the direc 
tors of the omployer company to extend the period of service . Before I proceed further 
to coasider this point, I need remind myself of certain observations of the Supreme Court 
in this context. In the case of Guest, Keen , Williams ( Private ) Ltd ., vs. Sterling ( P . J. ) 
und others. ( 1959 ) II LLJ 405 at page 415 , Gajendragadkar, J. ( as he then was ) 
obocrved : 
" In fixing the age of superannuation industrial tribunals have to take into account 

several relevant factors. What is the nature of the work assigned to the 
employees in the course of their employment? What is the nature of the 
wage structure paid to them ? What are the retirement benefits and other 
amenities available to them ? What is the character of the climate 
where the employees work ond what is the age of superannuation fixed in 
comparable industries in the same region ? What is generally the practice 
prevailing in the industry in the past in thc matter of retiring its employees ? 
These and other relevant facts have to be weighed by tho tribunal in cvery 
case when it is called upon to fix an age of superannuation in an industrial 
dispute." 


Later on , iu Dunlop Rubber Company (India ) , Ltd . and its worknien ( 1959 ) II LLJ 826 
at page 828 , Wanchoo , J. ( as he then was ) observed ; 
“ We may in this connection refer to a recent decision of this court in Guest, Keon , 

Williams (Private ) Ltd ., Calcutta v P . ) . Sterling and others ( 1959- II LLJ. 
405 ) , where the age of superannuation of employees in service before the 
standing orders came into force in that concern was fixed at 60 ycars . In 
thesc circumstances, if the tribunal thought that it would be fair to fix 60 
years as the age of retirement for clerical staff in spite of the fact that in 
the agreenit of 1956 the retirement age was fixed at 55 years , it cannot 
be said that the tribunal s order was not in accord with the prevailing condi 
tions in many concerns in that region ." 


Stull later on , in the case of Workinen of Jessop und Co. Ltd . and Jessop and Co . Lid ., and 
others, ( 1964 ) I LLJ 451 at pago 455, Wanchoo , J . ( as he then was ) again observed : 
" We feel however that the time has now come for increasing the age of retirement 

in the case of cic , ical staff and subordinate staff. The question was consi 
dered by this Court in Guest, Keen , Williams (Privatc ) , Ltd . v . P . J. Sterl 
ing and others ( 1959. II LLJ. 405 ) and the consideration to be taken into 
account in fixing the date of retirement were there indicated . Bearing 
those considerations in mind we are of opinion that age of retirement for 
clerical staff should be increased as a first step to 58 . " 


It is noteworthy that his Lordship stopped at 58 instead of going upto 60 only ay a 
first step . TË I have understood the judgement correctly , his Lordship meant to indicate 
that there was scope for further raising the retirement age. Lastly , in the case of 
British Paints ( India ) Ltd . is. Its u orkmen ( 1966 ) I LLJ 407 at page 409 , Wanchoo , 
1 . (a9 he then was ) said : 
" Considering that there has been a general iniprovement in the standaril of health 

in this country and also considering that longevity has increasod , fixation 
of age of retirement at 60 years appears to us to be quite reasonable in 
the present circumstances . Age of retirement at 55 years was fixed in the 
last century in Government servicc and had become the pattern for flxing 
the age of retirement everywhere . But time, in our opinion , has now come 
considering the improvement in the standard of health and increase in 
longevity in this country during the last Afty years that the age of retire 
ment should be fixed at a higher level, and we consider that , generally 
Speaking, in the present circumstances , fixing the age of retirement at 60 
years would be fair and proper , unless there are special circumstances 
justifying fixation of a lower age of retirement." 


$ 364 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : NOV . 23 , 1968 /AGRAHAYANA 2 , 1890 [Part 11 — 


Considering what has been stated by the Supreme Court and further considering that 
workmen , in an insurance company, are not required to do vigorous work and also con 
sidering that 60 years as the retiring age has been recognised in some of the insurance 
companics , say , in Hindustan General Insurance Society Limited and Indian Trade and 
General Insurance Company Limited , as appears from Ext. F and Ext. c , I think that 
the age of retirement, in the instant reference should be raised to 60 years. 
(iii) Sick leave : 


27 . Provision for sick leave , As contained in the service rule of the employer is set 
out below : 
" 2 (c ) Medical leave: Besides Privilege leave an employee may also be given 

Medical leave in caso of prolonged illness when the actual period of leave 
may not be covered by the Privilege leave , Medical Leave will be counted at 
the rate of 5 days per ytar of service . In case of an employee with 
service of ten years or more , in the event of serious and prolonged illness 
when the period is not covered by medical leave due, a further leave 
may also be granted to him for half the pumber of days of privilege leave 
which may not have availed of during the period of service but to which 
he was entitled. If the illness still persists such employce may be granted 
further leave at half pay for a period not exceeding 3 months and thereafter 
he may be granted further leave without pay for a further period of 9 

months. 
When an employce has suffered from such prolonged illness a certificate from a 

Doctor authorised for the purpose by the Company will be required to be 
produced before he rojoins. 


5 ( a ) Sick leave of short duration will be treated as casual leave but if there is no 

casual lcave due, it will be adjusted against accumulated privilege leave, iſ 

any . 
6 . When an employee after putting a long service falls a victim to illooss for a 

continuous long period, his case may be libcrally considered even if he may 
not have any accumulated leave at his crcdit at the time. Such leave if 

granted , will be treated as special leave and is not to be treated as precedent. 
7 . The above rules do not necessarily limit the discretionary powers of the 

management who may treat deserving cases liberally without establishing any 

precedent." 
It was contended , on behalf of the employer company, that regard being bad to the 
existence of other classes of leave granted to the employees, namely , privilege leave , 
casual leave and holidays , the extent of sick leave, as it now stands, should not be inter 
fered with . I am not satisfied . Sickness is a misfortune which may befall everybody. 
The consideration on which other classes of leave are granted and the consideration OD 
which sick leave need to be granted are different. Sickness is a compelling circumstance 
which physically disables a man and makes him take rest. Social justice demands that 

sick man must not be compelled to work , in sQ far as it can be helped . I do not 
mean to say that if a man falls sick and remains sick for a long time, he must have the 
right to sick leave until recovery . What I mean to say that some reasonable period of 
sick leave should be granted to all employees . Again , adjustment of short period of 
medical leave against casual leavc or privilege leave , as under Rule 5 ( a ) , seems to have 
little justice behind it. I, therefore , award that there should be at least 15 days Sick 
leave per year, on ful} pay , granted to each employeo . So much of Rules 2 ( c ) and 
$ ( a ) of the Service Rules of tho company as provide for adjustment of medical leave 
against casual leave or privilege leave shall no longer be enforcible and be deemed to 
bo non - existent. In Rule 2 ( c ) the extent of medical leave shall be raised to 13 days 
per year. The Rule , however , shall not otherwise be modified . Nothing herein contained 
shall interfere with the other discretionary or further sick leave , which the company now 
grants to the employees under its service rules. The period of sick Icave shall be allowed 
to be accumulated upto 45 ( forty - five ) days in the total. 


( iv ) House rent : 

28. Tho opinion generally expressed by the Supreme Court, say for example , in Parna 
Electric Supply Co . Ltd . vs. Paina Electric Supply Workers Union ( 1959 ) II LLJ, 366 . 
Tocklal Experimental Station Vs. Its workmen , ( 1961) II LL ) , 694 and Mohammed and 
Sons Vg. Their workmen ( 1968 ) I LLJ, 536 is that time has not matured to throw the 
burden of housing accommodation on the industry alone. Here , however, we are not con 
cerned with any case of housing accommodation but only with the claim of contribution 
towards house rent. The ground on which this demand is made is the general and the 
vague ground of ever -rising trend of house rent in towns and cities and also becauso such 
house rent is boing allowed by some progressive employers. This clajin was sought to be 


Sec . 3 (ii) ] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : NOV , 23 , 1968 /AGRAHAYANA 2, 1890 5365 


repelled with the contention that provision for house sent stood included in wages and 
need not be separately provided . 

29 . Now , in the case of Workinen of Calcuttu Electric Supply Co . Ltd . vs . Calcutta 
Electric Supply Corporalion ( 1952 ) LAC 280 , the Labour Appellate Tribunal observed 
that " the itern of house rent is ordinarily taken into account in fixing wages . . . . . . the in 
crcase in the house rent is also taken into account in fixing dearneys allowance ." Samo 
view also was repeated by the Labour Appellate Tribunal in the case of Workman of 
Karainchand Thapper and Bros. ys, Karamchand 7 huppar und Bros. ( 1953 ) LAC 152 , In 
the instant case , I find that in the recent judgment between the same parties the Supreme 
Court fixed a fair scale of dearness allowance for the workmen , and also approved of 
the wage scalçs as fixed . I do not think that the Supreme Court over looked considera : 
tions of house rent in doing so . l further find that excepting in cases of companies liko 
New India Jasurance Company Ltd . and Oriental Fire und General Insurance Co . Ltd ., 
other insurance companies are not giving anything towards house rent separately . How 
far the aforesaid two insurance companies are comparable units is debatable . I do not, 
therefore , think that this claim should be upheld at this stage. 

30 . In the result, ( a ) i disallow the claim for house rent allowance; ( b ) I modify the 
provision for gratuity , as indicated in paragraph 25 of the award ; ( c ) The age of retire 
ment is raised from 58 years to 60 yeurs , as in paragraph 26 of thc Award ; and ( d ) the 
period of Sick Icavo is also increased from 5 days to 15 days in a year , as in paragraph 
27 of the award . 
I make an award accordingly . 

(Sd .) B . N . BANERJEE , 

Presiding Officer

AWARD 
The Governincnt of India , Ministry of Labour and Employment have by their Ordor 
No . 70 / 2 / 07-LRIII , dated 2nd November, 1967 referred to this Tribunal in industrial 
disputo existing between the employers in relation to the Vulcan Insurance Company 
Ltd ., Bombay and their workmcn in respect of the subject matter specified in the follow 
ing schedule : 


SOM DULE 
" Whether the management of Messrs . Vulcan Insurance Company Limited , Bombay 

was justified in discharging Shri 1 . T . Bengali from service with effect from 

the 8th October, 1966 ? If not, to what relief is the workman entitled ?"" 
2 . The workman Shri D . T . Bengali was in the employ of the Vulcan Insurance Com 
pany Ltd . The company has by its written statement alleged that Shri Bengali was 
employed as general-clerk - cum -typist froni the year 1955 . He was a permanent employce 
and was attending to the work in the marine , fire and miscellaneous dopartments and was 
doing typing work also . His work was found unsatisfactory and he has been given oral 
warnings by his superiors to show improvement in his work . But in spite of the warningą 
no Improvement was noticed and in the month of April 1965 he was charge-sheetel for 
ncgligence in the performance of his duties and for remaining absent without leave but 
as he linconditionally apologised the proceedings were dropped . 

3 . It is alleged that the Sucul Branch of the company received one letter from Shri 
H . M . Shah , Bombay , intimating them of a marine claim . The letter contained the parti 
culins of goods lying in the Bombay Docks. It was the duty of Shri Bengnli to inform 
thc head office in Bombay immediately for deputing a gurveyor. But he neither attended 
to the letter nor did he hand over the letters to the officer -in - charge but went on leve 


Slc . 3 (11 ) ] THE GAZETTL OF INDJ1 NOV, 23, 1968 / AGRAHAYANA 2, 1890 5367 


Sccondiy Shri Bengali was requested on phone by the Central Bank of India LU ., 10 
rencw the fire policy occount of Messi s . Panna Knitting Industries , włuch was due on 
11th June 1966 but thc Policy was not issued by Shri Bengali, and when he was asked 
he took up an attitude of gross insubordination and wrote a letter to the Deputy Branch 
Secretary in offensive language and made serious allegations, 

4 . The Divisional Munager thoreupon lepoited to the head office of the company 
that this type of insubordination and negligcncc in duties on the part of Shri Bengali did 
not augur well for healthy atmosphere and discipline in the oflice and suggested necessary 
action to be taken against him whereupon the head office called upon Shri Bengali to 
give a written cxplanation for negligence of his duties and for insubordination . He was 
charyc -sheetcd and an enquiry was held against him . Though Shri Bengali was present 
at the time of the enquiry he refused to participate in it and the enquiry was held ex- parte . 
The enquiry officer found that Shri Bengali was guilty of the misconducts and he sub 
mitted his report to the management holding that the charges lovelled against Shri Bengali 
were proved . Thereupon the management informed Shri Bengali by its letter dated 5th 
October 1966 that he was discharged from service and that he would be paid one month s 
wages. 


5 . The workman Shri Bengali who is a meinber of the Vulcan Insurance Company Ltd . 
Staff Union has by his written Statement denied all the allegations and has contended that 
he was appointed a portanont employee of the company as a typist apd it was not nis 
duty to do any other work which was to be executed by oficors acting in a managerial 
capacity . He has alleged that in the year 1965 when the applicant became a member of 
the General Insurance Employees Union at Abmedabad the opponent becamc displeased 
with him and ever since till the discharge from service tho company developed an unhappy 
attitude towards him . He has denied the allegations about negligence and has contended 
that the chai ye sheet was null and void inasmuch as the duties refered to werc the duties 
involving managerial functions or the functions of officers . He was merely a typist und 
he had nothing to do with the discharge of the duties and responsibilities referred to in 
the charge -sheets . He has also alleged that hte management was motivated by mala fide 
intentions. The enquiry was not proper and this Tribunal should set aside the order of 
discharge and reinstate the workman . 

6 . When the matter was fixed for hearing the parties negotiated the dispute and it 
was therefore adjourned for settlement. They have settled the matter amicably and have 
Aled an application dated 18th October 1968 togсther with the terms of settlement praying 
that an award may be in terms of tho same. 

7 . According to the terms of the settlement the workmen Shri D . T . Bengali is to be 
treated as having resigned from the service of the company with effcct from 8th October 
1966 and in view of this the order dated 5th October, 1966 terminating his services shall 
be treated as withdrawn . The company has also agreed to pay Shri Bengali his salary 
from 1st to 8th October 1966 , his grutuity , bontis , provident fund including the company s 
contribution , leavc salary and an additional pr- gratia payment totalling Rs. 5 , 000 . The 
terms of settlement are signed by the General Manager and Advocate for the company , 
the General Secretary and Vice- President of the Union and Shri D . T . Bengali himself . 
Shri Bengali was working as a typist and as ho is deemed to have resigned from service 
there is no question of any stiam to he attached to th - tarmination of his services . In 

on the terms of settlement are fair and reasonable and 1 aceng ** 
pass an award in terms of the said settlement annexuie A which shall form part of this 
award . 


No order as to costs .

AWARD 
The Government of India in its Ministry of Labour, Employment and Rehabilitation 
(Department of Labour And Employment) had , by order No . 28 / 106 / 67 -LR .ILI dated 
20th October 1967 , referred this disputo to me for adjudication . Tho issue as per schedulo 
annored to tho notification 19 this ; 
Whother the iron and steel handling labour at the Port of Visakhapatnam are 

entitled to the payment of bonus under the Payment of Bonus Act, 1965 
for the accounting years commencing on any date in 1964 , 1965 and 1966 
from the Iron and Steel Handling Contractors at the Port of Visakhapatnam ? 

If so , at what rate ? 
2 . The Port Khalasis Union , Visakhapatnam , is party to the reference . To start with , 
three of the employers , viz ., The Visakhapatnam Minerals Association , Visakhapatnam 
Port, Monsrs . K . Ramabrahmam and Sons and Messro, Dhanareddy And Company, are 
party to the reference . By a subsequent notification under the samo roferenco the Govorn 
wont of India had , by order dated 29th November 1967, imploaded soven more employers 
to the reference. They are ( 1 ) Messrs. Roy and Chatterjee Private Ltd ., ( 2 ) Messrs. P . V . 
Ramanamurty, (3 ) Messrs . Shreeram Shipping Service (P ) Limited , (4 ) Messrs. La Rive 
and Company, (5 ) Mo8919. G . S . Murty and Ch . Agashthayya and Company , (6 ) Mogers, 
International Shippling Corporation and (7 ) Messrs. A . V . Bhanojirow , G . P . Ramayya 
and Company (P ) Limited . 

3 . The statement of claims was filed by and under tho signature of Mr. P . M . Naidu , 
Prosident of the Port Khalasis Union, Visakhapatnam . The case of the claimants is that 
they are employees of the Contractor - employers who handle iron and steel and that thore 
fore they are entitled to bonus from them for the three years under claim , viz ., 1964, 
1965 and 1966 . Admittedly , any bonus was not paid to any of the claimants for any 
of the years under claim . This claim is for payment of minimum bonus as prescribed 
by the Bonus Act, 1965 . This claim is on par with similar payment to other classos of 
employees at the Visakhapatnam Port . 

4 . The various contractors , whose names I had set out earlier , had formed themselves 
into a body called the Shipping Employers Federation which was registered As far back 
us in the year 1956 . The Federation itself is not as such party to the reference. But, 
howevei, these various employers who are individually party to the reference had 
authorised the Federation to filo counter on their behalf. The counter was filed by and 
under the signature of Mr. D . Rama Mohana Rao who is the Honorary Secretary of the 
Pederation The counter puts the claimants to proof that there is employer-employce 
releationship between the contractors on the one side and the claimants on the other. It is 
denied that there was any such relationship during the period of the three years under 
claim . That being so , it is pointed out that the claim for payment of bonus is not 
tenable 

5 . One witness is examined on behalf of the claimants as W . W . 1 . He is Mr. P . M . 
Naidu , President of the Port Khalasis Union , Visakhapatnam , that being the only Union 
that is party to the reference . One witness is examined on behalf of the Shipping 
Employers Federation as M . W . 1. He is Mr. D . Rama Mohana Rao who is the 
Honorary Secretary of the Federation . Exs. W 1, W 2 and W 3 are marked on the side 
of the claimants . Any document is not tendered in evidence on behalf of the employers. 
I heard arguments of Mr. V . Jagannadha Rao of the claimants and of Mr. D . Satya 
narayana for the omployers. 


6 . The issue does not specify the different classes of workers who constitute the 
general designation of iron and steel handling labour. W . W . I made this clear. Ho 


SEC , 3 ( 11 ) ] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : NOV. 23 , 1968 / AGRAHAYANA 2 , 1890 5373 


tostificd that they were mazdoors , maistries, tindals, tally clerks , carpenters, carpenter 
helpers and markers working on cranes , i.c ., workers on the mechanical side . The witness 
said that all these categories who so constitute the iron and steel workers on the mechanical 
side are members of tho Port Khalasis Union of which he is the President. Even from 
the inception of the period under claim the claimants had been putting forward demands 
from timo to time for payment of bonuş aş testificd by Mr. Naldu (W . W . 1) . The last 
of such demand is Ex. W 2 dated 17th July 1967. The counter does not gainsay this 
periodic persistence in the demand for payment of bonus , W . W . 1 said that the claimants 
are the employees of thç contractors who had formed themselves into Shipping Employers 
Federation . This testimony by W . W . 1 that there was employer employee relationship 
between the contractors and the claimants is not gainsaid in Cross - examination . Indeed , 
there was no cross - examination of this witness. Mr. Satyanarayana, Advocate representing 
the omployers, said that there was no cross -examination . But M . W . 1, Mr. D . Rama 
Mohana Rao , who is the Honorary Secretary of the Shipping Employers Federation , said 
that prior to the year 1967 there was no employer-employee relationship between the 
Federation and the Contractors on the one side and the claimants on the othor . Nothing 
has been asked to W . W . 1 by way of any cro9 $ -examination suggesting any factor 
circumstance from which it could be inferred that thoro was no such employer - cmpiui , 
relationship . Presumably the reason why Mr. Ramamohanarao testified as above is that 
for the first time it was early in the year 1967 that the Federation as such had taken 
over formation of a pool of workers of the kind under mention and had laid down tbo 
procedure in respect thereof . This is seen from Ex . W 3 dated 5th March 1967 which 
was a settlement under section 12( 3 ) of the Industrial Disputes Act between the Federation 
and the individual contractors on the one side and the Port Khalasis Union on the 
other representing the iron and steel handling workers. That document was regarding tho 
formation and working of the pool of these workers. The idea behind tho formation 
of a pool of tho kind under mention seems to have occurred to the Federation and to 
its Contractor members on the analogy of the dock workers pool maintained by the 
Dock Labour Board there . For the more reason that it was only by Ex . W 3 that the 
Foderation had formally taken over control of the workers by forming a pool as referred 
to , it does not mean that so far as the workers are concerned they were not tho employees 
of the individual Contractors who are Members of the Federation . M . W . 1 said that 
prior to the year 1967 the work gangs were recruited by the maistries, and that therefore 
it should be assumed that prior to tho year 1967 tho workers in question woro not tho 
employees of the individual contractors. For every gang of mazdoors there would be 
at least one maistry , the maistry himself being an employee of the contractor. A maistry 
is a necessity where a large number of workers are employed . The existence of a maistry 
does not effeot a breach in the employer -employee relationship . A maistry does some sort 
of supervisory work . 

7 . We have Ex. W 1 dated 24th March 1965. It was a settlement under section 
12 ( 3 ) of the 1. D . Act between the Shipping Employers Federation on the one side and 
the iron and steel handling workers on the other represented by the Port Khalasia Union . 
One of the clauses in that document is that the Shipping Employers Federation would 
register the iron and steel handling workers . W . W . 1 testified that it was effected within 
two months following that document. Thus it is seen that even as carly as on 24th March 
1965 the Federation had recognised that the iron and steel handling workers were the 
employecs of the contractors who are members of that Federation ; otherwise such a positive 
step would not have been taken by the Federation on behalf of its constituent members . 
Unless the employer -employce relationship had been there even from prior to 24th 
March 1965 , the Federation would not have felt obliged to agree to what it did as per 
Ex, W 1 . Having regard to the facts and the circumstances in the case , I have no doubt 
in holding that there was employer-employee relationship between the Federation and its 
contractor members on the one side and the claimants on the other, the latter being the 
iron and stccl handling workers on the mechanical side working on tho cranes. They 
are , us stated by W . W . 1, mazdoors, maistries, tindals , tally clerks , carpenters , carponter 
helpers and markers. It further follows that these workers who are the claimants in this 
casc are cntitled to bonus . The claimants have taken care to put their demand on par 
with what had been given to other classes of employees in the Visakhapatnam Port Trust, 
that being the minimum bonus prescribed under the Bonus Act. Mr. R . M . Naidu 
( W . W . 1) testified that such is the demand . It is no doubt true that the Bonus Act was 
not there in the year 1964. Even for the year 1964 the bonus claimed is the minimum 
as prescribed in the Bonus Act. Prior to thc Bonus Ordinance it was the L . A . T . formula 
that held the field on the question of bonus. It is not suggested to W . W . 1 in cross 
examination that bonus for the year 1964 would be less than the minimum prescribed 
by the subsequent Bonus Act or that nothing would have been payable if the L. A . T . 
formula had been made applicable for that year. Therefore the least that can be said 
for the claim for the year 1964 is that oven if the L . A . T , formula had been made applicablo 
for that year , the bonus payable would not have been less than the minimum prescribed 
by the Bonus Act. 


- - - 


3574 THE GAZEITE OF INDIA : NOV . 23 , 1968/ AGRAHAYANA 2 . 1890 [PART 11 

- - - - 

- - - - - 
8 . Having regard to the facts and the circumstances in the case and to the evidence 
let in before me, my finding under the issue as per schedule annexed to tļie notification 
is that the iron and steel handling labour at the Visakhapatnam Pori, viz ., mazdoors . 
maistries, tindals , tally clerks , carpenters , carpenter - helpers and markets on the mechanical 
side, ie , working on the cranes , are entitled to the minimum bonus prescribed under the 
Bonus Act 1965 for the accounting years commencing on any date in 1964, 1965 and 
1966 from the Irom and Steel Handling Contractors at the Port of Visakhapatnam . 

Award passed accordingly , 

Given under my hand and the seal of the Tribunal, this the 28th day of September 
1968 . 

Sd.) M . NAJMUDDIN , 

Industrial Tribunal.

The Government of India , Ministry of Labour and Employment have by their Order 
No. 28 / 76 /67- LRUIT dated 31st October, 1967 referred to this Tribunal an industrial dis 
pute existing between the employers in relation to the Bombay Stevedores Association 
Linvited and their workmen in respect of the matters specifled in the following schedulo : 

SCHEDULE 
" Whether tho Bombay Stovedores Association Limited , Bombay was justified in 

terminating the services of Shri J. D . Silva , Upper Division Clerk , with effect 
from 28th February 1967 ? Tt not, to what relief is he entitled ?" 


Str . 3 (1 ) ] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : NOV, 23 , 1968 / AGRAHAYANA 2 , 1890 5377 


2 . The workman Shri J. D . Silva who is a member of the Transport and Dock 
Workers Union had been in the employ of the Bombay Stevedores Association Limited 
for the last 18 years. The Union has by its written statement alleged that on or about 
the 28th February 1967 the Secretary of the Association by his letter to the workman 
informed him that his behaviour and conduct as an employee of the Association was not 
congenial to the discipline and decorum of the office and his services were terminated 
with immediate effect. The Union has alleged that the workman was dismissed from 
service for the alleged misconduct. No charge -sheet was issued to the workman and no 
enquiry was held against him and the Association had not carricd out its function in the 
matter of disciplinary action of a permancat employce and the order of dismissal was 

ilegal. After the dismissal of the workman they had made representations to the 
Association but it did not pay any attention . The matter was also referred to the 
Conciliation Officer but the Association was adamant and stuck to its stand that the 
action taken by them was legal and proper and hence the Concillation Officer sont a 
failure report and as a result this dispute has been referred for adjudication . 


3 . After the receipt of the order of reference notices were issued to both the parties 
And the union filed its statement contending that the dismissal was illegal. But the 
Stevedores Association after various adjournments did not file any reply . When the matter 
was fixed for hearing the parties negotiated the dispute . The workman was also present. 
The Union was represented by Advocate Sowani. The particg arrived at a setttlement 
and made an application praying for an award in terms of the same. 


und and heat workmanoinclaim agaimany other 


4 . By the settlement the Association has agreed to pay to the workman Rs. 4500 / 
ex- gratia in addition to the provident fund and gratuity to which he was eligible . The 
above ex - gratia payment is to be accepted by the workman in full and floal settlcment 
of his claims against the Association and he would have no claim against the Bombay 
Stevedores Association (Administrative Body ) either for reinstatement or any other claim 
Considering the circumstances that the Association has agreed to pay a large sum of 
Rs. 4500 / - as ex -gratia to the workman and considering the status of the partics and 
the circumstances I think the terms of settlement are fair and reasonable and pass an 
award in terms of the settlement annexure A which shall form part of this Award . 
No order as to costs.

ago Corporation , Cochin and their workmen, which was received by the Central Govern

BEFORE THE CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL , BOMBAY

STATT Kerala 

INDUSTRY: Major Ports and Docks, 
Bombay dated 1912 October 1968 

AWARD PART 1 
The Government of India , Ministry of Labour and Employment have by their Order 
No. 28 /60 /64 -LRIV dated 13th November 1964 referred to this Tribunal for adjudication 
an industrial dispute existing between the employers in relation to ( 1 ) Sbri Paul Abrao 
of M / s . Paul Abrao and Sons, Willingdon Island , Cochin and ( 2 ) the Cochin Lighterage 
Corporation , Cochin , on the one part and their wothmen on the other in respect of the 
mations specified in the following schedulc : - - 

SCHEDULE 
| Whether the action of the employers, ( 1 ) Shri Paul Abrao of Messrs . Paul 

Abrao and Sons, Cochin , and ( 2 ) the Cocliin lightcial Corporation , Cochin 
1, in not providing work for the workmen specified in Schedule IT who were 


Sec . 3 (ii) ] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : NOV . 23, 1968 / AGRAHAYANA 2 , 1890) 5379 


engaged in floating craft prior to and as on 1st June 1962 or theroafter, is 

justified ? 
" 2. If not, to what relief are the workmen cntitled and from which of the two 

employers ?" 
2 . There are about 164 workmen involved in this dispute who have been specified in 
Schedule II which is attached hereto as appendix 1. These workmen aro 1epresented by 
the two unions ( 1 ) The Cochin Port Labour Union, Cochin 1 and ( 2 ) The Cochin Thura 
mugha Thozhilali Union , Cochin 2 . Out of them 122 workmen specified in list 1 were 
employed prior to 1st June, 1962 while the remaining 42 from list 2 have been employed 
after 1st June , 1962. 

3 . The main dispute referred for adjudication is in respect of the denial of employment 
to the workmen specified in the schedule and their claim for compensation for tho period 
of non - employment. The Cochin Port Cargo Labour Univu has by its written statement 
contended that the workers were in the employ of Paul Abrao & Sons, employer No . 1 
as tindals and lascars in his floating craft since long. In the year 1962 Paul Abrao had 
entered into an arrangement with the Cochin Lightcrage Corporation employer No. 2 
regarding the management of the floating crafts retaining the ownership of the crafts with 
him and deriving income from the business. This arrangement between the oinployers was 
not genuine and made with tho sole motive to defeat the rights of the workinen and evade 
the various taxes. During the year 1963 both the employers No. 1 and 2 began to deny 
employment to the workmen shown in schedule ) I by docking the boats under the excuse 
of repairs part by part. They did not have the intention to repair the boats and the boats 
were purposely kept in the docks without attending to repairs which was deliberately done 
to deny cmployment to the workmen . The union protested to the employers against the 
denial of employment to the workmen but even after a series of conferences to settle the 
disputes the management did not take any stops to repair the boats and the union had 
finally to raise the dispute for reiterating their demands for employment and compensation . 
It is alleged that the employer No. 2 the Cochin Lighterage Corporation is not an inde 
pendent body . It has no finances. It is the agent of employer No. l. The employer 
No. 1 is getting the work done by hiring boats from other boat owners which work was 
to be done by the workmen and the non -employment is mula fide and the workers are 
entitled to be reinstated and to be pail compensation for the period of non -employment. 

4 . The other union the Cochin Thurmugha Thozhilali Union has by its written state 
ment contended that the floating crafts in which the workmen were employed originally 
belonged to Shri Paul Abrao who on 1st June, 1962 is said to have sold and transferred 
them to the Cochii Lighterags Corporation and the Corporation has taken over the entire 
going concern but subscquently thc boats and crafts were kept idle and the workers were 
denied employment and the employer liable should be directed to pay the wagcs and 
compensation to the workmen . 

5 . Employer No . 1 Shri Paul Abrao has opposed the references contending that the work 
mcn specified in schedule II were not in his employ and there was no cinployer - employee 
relationship between them and the reference was bad in law and ultra vires of the powers 
of the Central Government. Shri Paul Abrao has alleged that in the year 1962 he had 
sold and transferred to the Cochin Lighterage Corporation his boats and floating crafts as 
a going concern with a goodwill of the entire business. Thereafter the Cochin Lighterage 
Corporation was carrying on the business of operating the boats and floating crafts and 
Paul Abrao had absolutely no interest or any right or control in the affairs of the Cochin 
Lighterage Corporation . 

6 . Shri Paul Abrao has further contended that the Cochin Lighterage Corporation has 
heen mismanaging its business and was unable to operate the boats and provide employ 
ment to its workmen and there was also a settlement between the Cochin Lighterage Cor 
poration and its workmen but even after the same the Corporation had failed to provide 
work and the union resorted to direct action and other measures against them . But now 
the partners of thc Cochin Lighterage Corporation and the office bearers of the union 
representing the workmen have in collusion put forward a false claim at the Cochin 
Lighterage Corporation and its business belonged to Shri Paul Abrao and that he was the 
employer of the workmen cmployed by the Cochin Lighterage Corporation . He has con 
tended that Shri Paul Abreo is not the employer of thc workmen concerned and no relief 
can be granted against him . There was no industrial dispute in respect of the matters 
specified in schedule 1 and the reference against him should be rejected . 

7 . The Cochin Lightcrage Corporation employer No. 2 has by its written statement 
contended that it was a partnership of the close relation of Shri Paul Abrao formed at his 
instance . But it was Shri Paul Abrao who had been carrying on the business of shipping , 
clearing and forwarding. Shri Paul Abrao had employed some office bearers and other 
relatives in his business in various capacities. But in the year 1962 Shri Paul Abrao had 
taken initiative and had formed the Cochin Lighterage Corporation for thic adıinistration

14 . The preliminary points that arise for my decision are :

15 . Shri T . C . N , Menon , learned Counsel for the Union has argued that the question 
of ernployer - employee relationship will not go to the root of the reference and it is un

and the reference regarding employer No. I will be bad and without jurisdiction . 


16 . It has been held in the above rug that Government acting under section 10 is 
doing an administrative act. . . . and while it will be open to a party to impugn an award 
on the ground that what was referred was not an industrial dispute and fuct of its existence 
and propriety of reference were matters entirely for Government and a Court cannot quash

puted fact and a decision about it in favour of the cmployer is likely to save him from 
Turther stages of the proceedings and as the prelimirary points raised will go to the root 
of the matter I think it proper to decide these questions before recording evidence . 

17 . lio Cochin Lighterage Corporation came into existence somewhere in June 1962 
and starting looking after the shipping business carried on with the help of serangs, drivers , 
dascars and other workers . Employer No. 1 Shri Paul Aliao had by an indenturo assigned 
his cargo boats, barges and tugs as a going concern to this Corporation . Subsequently 
there arose disputes between the workmen , the serangs, drivers, lascars etc ., and the manage 
ment of the Cochin Lighterage Corporation which were referred to the Conciliation OlHcer . 
Some of the employees had also resorted to Satyagraha in front of the house of Paul Abro 
and hence Paul Abrao had filed two civil suits Nos. 0 . $ . 27 of 1964 und 55 of 1964 
against the Cochin Port Cargo Labour Union and its Secretary for an injunction restraining 
Athem and all other persons acting on their behalf from offering satyagraha . Some other 
persons have filed suits against the Corporation for recovery of rent eic ., and the legal 
Tepresentatives of Paul Abrao have row contended that the question about the alleged 
employer - employee relationship between Paul Abrao and the workmen has been decided 
in the above suits in favour of the cmployer . It has been held in those suits that there is 
no relationship between them and the question has become res judicata . In support of this 
cortention they have produced the pleadings and judgments in Suits Nos. 27 of 1964 
and SS or 1954 and have further produced the judgments in Suit Nos. S . C . 38 of 1965 
and O .S . 629 of 1965 . In the Grst suit which was heard ex parte it has been held : - - 
" The only proper course in this circumstance is to believe the plɔintiff s cases under 

Order 17 Rule 3 C .P . C . and to hold that the first defendant union represented 
by the second defendant was fully aware of the separation of the interests 
between the plaintiff in and the Cochin Lighterage Corporation and that 
the plaintiff firm is not the employer of the labourers represented by the 

first defendant union ." 
In the second suit which was also hcard ex parte it has been held 

" Counsel reported today that he had no instructions and the defendants being absent 

were declared or purte. Plaintiff s right is proved by P . W . 1 and exhibits P . 1 

to P . 8 . Hence the suit is decreed in terms of the plaint. " 
and the defendants in both the suits were restrained from offering satyagraha and the 
question is whether the finding in these two suits that the plaintiffs Messrs. Paul Abrao 
are not the employers of the labourers represented by the union has become res judicata 
and cannot be considered again in this proceeding. 

18 . Shri V . K . K . Menon the learned Counsel on behalf of Shri Paul Abrao s legal 
representatives has argued that the main issue in the present reference is the cmployer 
employee relationship between the parties, the principles of res judicata are made applicable 
to industrial disputes and in view of the decisions of the Civil Courts in the two suits it is 
not now open to the employees to raise the same contention in this reference . The leurned 
Counsel has relied upon the rulings reported in 1957 1 LLJ 226 , AIR 1961 ( 1 ) S .C . 1457 , 
1965 II LLJ p . 83 and A . I. R . 1964 S . C . 322 and has further argued that though the issue 
has been previously decided by the Civil Courts they were competent to try the suits con 
cerned and the decision has become res judicata on the grounds of public policy. The 


538 .2 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : NOV. 23 , 1968 / AGRAHAYANA 2 , 1890 [PART 11 


principles regarding res judicata are contained in section 11 of the Civil Procedure Code. 
There is no provision in the Industrial Disputes Act similar to section 11 of tho Civil 
Procedure Code. However, it is clear from the Supreme Court ruling reported in 1957 
1 LL p . 226 Burn & Co ., that the principles of res judicata have been made applicable to 
industrial disputes . Their Lordships of the Supreme Court have observed in this case : - - 
" But we propose to consider the question on the footing that there is nothing in the 

Statute to indicate the grounds on which an award could bo reopened . What 
then is the position ? Are we to hold that a award given on a matter in 
controversy between the parties after full hearing ceases to have any force 
if either of them repudiates it under section 19 ( 6 ) and that the tribunal has 
no option when the matter is again roferred to it for adjudication but to 
proceed to try it de novo , traverse the entiro ground once again and come 
to a fresh decision . That would be contrary to the well recognize principle 
that a decision once rendered by a competent authority on a matter in issue 
between the parties after a full enquiry should not be permitted to be re 
agitated . It is on this principle that the rule of res Judicata enacted in S . u 
of the Civil Procedure Code is based . That section is no doubt in terms 
inapplicable to the present matter but the principle underlying it expressed 
in the maxim interest rei publicae ut sit finis litiunt is founded on sound 
public policy and is of universal application . The rule of res judicara is 
dictated observed Sir Lawrence- Jenkins C .v . in Sheoparsan Singh v . Ram 
nandan Prasad Singh ( 1916 L . R . 43 LA . 91) " by a wisclom which is for all 
time" . And there are good reasons why this principle should be applicable 

to decisions of industrial tribunals also ." 
19, There arc similar observations about the applicability of the principles of res judicata 
in the other rulings and it shall have to be held that as per decisions in these cases the 
principles of res judicutu 210 applicablc to industrial disputes and the further question is 
whether the decisions of the Civil Courts quoted above satisfy all the requirements for the 
operation of the principles of res judicata and whether these findings of the Civil Courts 
would be binding in this reference . 

20 . Suit No . 27 of 1964 has been decided by the Subordinate Judge , Cochin and the 
judgment in Suit No . 55 of 1964 , has been given by the Principal Subordinate Judge, 
Ernakulam and it cannot be disputed that these Judges were competent to decido suits for 
injunctions . However , Shri T . C . N . Menon the learned Counsel for the Union has con 
tended that even if it be held that the principles of res judicuta are applicable to industrial 
disputes what would be binding on this Tribunal would be the decision or award given by 
a Tribunal in a previous industrial dispute between the same parties. Tho decrees of the 
Civil Courts are not awards passed under the Industrial Disputes Act. The Civil Courts 
havo no jurisdiction to decide industrial disputes and the findings of the Civil Courts 
cannot be considered by this Tribunal while adjudicating a dispute under the Industrial 
Disputes Act. 

21 . Shri V . K . K , Menon , the learned Counsel for Messrs . Paul Abruo has argued that 
in this case the contention regarding applicability of the plca of res judicalu is based on 
general principles of law and not under section 11 of the Civil Procedure Code which 
l equires that the Court that tried the previous suit must be competent to try the subsequent 
suit and though the Civil Court has no jurisdiction under the Industrial Disputes Act the 
finding will be binding . The learned Counsel has invited my attention to the observations 
of their Lordships of the Supreme Court in Srimati Raj Lakshmi Dasi and others 
Appellants v . Banamali Sen and others reported in 1953 S . C . 33. In this ruling it has 
been observed : 
" The condition regarding the competency of the former court to try the subsequent 

suit is one of the limitations engrafted on the general rule of res judicara by 
Section 11 of the Code and has application to suits alone. When a plea 
of res judicutu is founded on general principles of law all that is necessary 
to establish is that the Court that heard and decided the former casc was a 
Court of compctent jurisdiction . It does not seem nccessary in such cases 
to further prove that it has jurisdiction to hear the latter suit. A plea of res 
judicata on general principles can be successfully taken in respect of judgments 
of Courts of exclusive jurisdiction like revenuç courts, land acquisition courts ; 

administrative courts etc , 
22 . I do 101 think that this ruling will be applicable to the facts of our case . It is 
clear from the judgmenų that though the former court had no jurisdiction to try the sub 
sequent suit as the basis of res judicata is the finding of a civil court before another civil 
court it is held to be res judicata . The plea was put forth before another civil court having 
similar kind of judicial powers . The rule cannot be made applicable to finding of a 
civil court before not even an industrial court but an industrial tribunal which has only 
quasi-judicial powers. 


SEC. 3 ( 1) ] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : NOV , 23 , 1968 /AGRAHAYANA 2, 1890 5383 


23 . I have already quoted the observations of their Lordships of tho Supreme Court 
reported in 1957 I LLJ P 226 which is the first case by which the principles of res judicata 
were held to be applicablc to industrial disputes . The last sentence in that ruling is very 
significant in which their Lordships have stated : 
" And there are good reasons why this principle should be applicable to decisions of 

Industrial Tribunals also ." 
From this it is clear that for the principles of res judicata to be made applicable the deci 
sion must be that of an Industrial Tribunal. It is not in dispute that the powers of Civil 
Courts are quite different from those of an industrial tribunal. The Civil Court determines 
the questions about the rights and liabilities between two litigating parties . Those rights 
may arise from contracts or wills or inheritance etc ., and the Court has to consider only 
the rights and liabilities of the parties. Moreover civil courts have to work within the 
framework of the procedure prescribed for that purpose . 

24 . An industrial tribunal is the creation of a special statute the Industrial Disputes 
Act. The Tribunal is to follow the law laid down by the Statute and adopt the proceduro 
governed by the principles of natural justice . Though the tribunal works as a judicial budy 
the powers of the Industrial Tribunal are very different from those of the ordinary Civil 
Courts. It has jurisdiction and powers to give relief which Civil Courts do not possess . 
The jurisdiction of the Industrial Tribunal necessarily extends and includes powers to 
investigate and adjudicate unfettcred by the considerations based on contractual rights as 
betwecu the workmen and employers. The nature and scope of the jurisdiction of tho 
Tribunal differs from that of a civil court. The powers of the Industrial ) ribunal are 
wider. Under the circumstances only the award or decision of an Industrial Tribunal 
will be binding upon another industrial tribunal and the finding of a Civil Court referred 
to in A1. R . 1953 will not be binding on a tribunal and the ruling will not be applicable 
to industrial disputes . 

25 . The case reported in 1957 1 LLJ p . 226 has been later on discussed in the ruling 
reported in 1962 1 LLJ 261 in which the head note runs : 
" The principles enunciated in the decision in 1957 1 LIJ 226 (the applicability of 

the principle of res judicata to industrial adjudication ) would have upplication 
only when there is an adjudication by an industrial tribunal or jabour court 

on the merits of the dispute in question." 
26 . This would also support the view that a finding to be res judicata in a later refer 
cnce must be one given by an Industrial Tribunal in a previous dispute and not by a 
Civil Court. 


26A . Shri V . K . K . Menon , the learned Counsel for Shri Paul Abrao s legal represen 
tatives has next invited my attention to the ruling reported in 1965 II LL ) 483. However, 
this ruling also does not lay down that the decision of a Civil Court will be res judicata 
and binding on the Tribunal while adjudicating an industrial dispute . In this case ( Bijay 
Cotton Mills Ltd ., and Rashtriya Mill Mazdoor Sangh , Bijaynagar and others ) it has been 
observed : 
" The basis on which the principle of res judicata reşts is founded on considerations 

of public policy . It cannot be treated as irrelevant or inadmissible in indus. 
trial adjudications. In tho instant case the industrial tribunal who made the 
prior award was a tribunal of co -ordinate jurisdiction . The prior award was 
not modified or reversed . . . . the industrial tribunal in the instant case should 

not have gone into the question once again ." 
This would show that the plea of res judicata was based upon a prior award given by an 
industrial tribunal and not on the finding of a Civil Court and I do not think that this 
will help the cmployers. 

27. The learned Counsel for the Union has further contended that even if all the other 
points are held against the workmen still as tho suits filed in the civil courts were not 
between the same parties between whom the present dispute is pending the findings of the 
civil court will not be binding and the plea of res judicata cannot be upheld . It has been 
argued that the previous suits were between the employers on the one hand and the union 
on the other while the present dispute is between the employers and the workinon and as 
the proceedings are not between the same parties the contention about res judicata cannot 
be considered . 

28. The learned Counsel for the employers has argued that though in the previous guit 
the union was the defendant in fact the suit was against the workmen and even the union 
treated the suit As ons against the workmen . Th¢ learned Counsel has read to me som 
paragraphs from the plaint and written statement and has relied upon explanation VI to 
section 11 of the Civil Procedure Code in which it has been stated 
" Where persons litigatę bona fide in respect of a public right or of a private right 

claimed in common for themselves and others all persong interested in such
